# Catalogue of the databases

The kinds and titles of each database. Databases 1 to 34 are written by hand; the databases from 35 on are generated from the record files by `tools/build_catalogue.py`, between the markers at the end of this file.

The catalogue of database 1 (80s) is split by kind of record:

| Kind | Records | What it is |
|---|---:|---|
| Languages | 56 | Languages of the 1950s–80s, from mass-market to almost forgotten |
| Systems | 48 | Operating systems, Unix variants, research environments, DBMSs, networks |
| Devices | 99 | Mainframes, Soviet machines, PCs, home computers, processors, peripherals |
| Tools | 33 | make, lint, yacc, GCC, editors, debugger, TeX, sed and others |
| Practices | 60 | Methods, design, testing, standards, processes, BBS and demoscene culture |
| Guides | 5 | How pipelines, recursive descent, the Forth stack, Makefiles and emulators work |
| Examples | 5 | Short recipes: word frequencies, sed, grep on logs, qsort, GROUP BY |
| Projects | 4 | Game of Life in awk, a maze in C, an address book in sh, a yacc calculator |
| Flex | 5 | The maximum from the minimum: a fractal with one operator, a quine, Mandelbrot in awk |
| Cross-code | 5 | Experiments with mixed languages: C with awk, make with awk, Forth inside C, bc with awk |

The catalogue of database 2 (90s) has the same ten kinds:

| Kind | Records | What it is |
|---|---:|---|
| Languages | 20 | Java, Perl, Python, Ruby, PHP/FI, JavaScript, Visual Basic 4, Delphi, C++98, and some forgotten languages |

| Devices | 32 | Pentium, PowerPC 601, Alpha, consoles, graphics and sound cards, handhelds, clusters |
| Tools | 24 | CVS, Perforce, Vim, NetBeans, Cygwin, trackers, Winamp, mIRC, ICQ, zlib and others |
| Practices | 20 | Extreme Programming, Scrum, design patterns, UML, the CHAOS report, COCOMO II |
| Guides | 5 | How CGI, Java bytecode, a BSP tree, the mode 13h screen and Winsock work |
| Examples | 7 | Visit counter, word count with Hashtable, PHP table, SQL join, DOS backup |
| Projects | 4 | A web server in Perl, an ASCII raycaster, a WAD lister, a BBS door game |
| Flex | 5 | Mandelbrot in JavaScript, a Perl quine, compile-time primes, plasma and fire |
| Cross-code | 5 | Java calling C through JNI, PHP calling C, Perl writing a C header, Lua in C, Visual Basic calling a DLL |

The catalogue of database 9 (2026 to 2036) is a forecast, not a record. Its languages, systems, devices, tools and practices are guesses, and their details say what they rest on. The guides, examples, projects, flex pieces and cross-code experiments are working code for the techniques behind the forecast. In this database the rare flag marks the long shots. It has the same ten kinds:

| Kind | Records | What it is |
|---|---:|---|
| Languages | 6 | Carbon, static reflection in C++, value classes in Java, the free-threaded Python build, tool calls checked by the compiler and quantum circuits in ordinary compilers |
| Systems | 5 | Post-quantum key exchange in TLS, WebAssembly components as plug-ins, Rust drivers in the Linux kernel, quantum cloud runtimes and post-quantum firmware signing |
| Devices | 8 | AR glasses for developers, a fault-tolerant quantum computer with 200 logical qubits, a neural accelerator in every laptop, a humanoid home robot, a neuromorphic PCIe card, a RISC-V laptop, a co-packaged optical switch and a wristband for input |
| Tools | 6 | Prompt linters and tests in CI, a proof assistant for everyday functions, signed bills of materials checked on install, a carbon meter in CI, post-quantum signatures in package managers and a sandbox for every tool call |
| Practices | 5 | A crypto inventory and crypto agility, agent access reviews, spec before code with agents, local-first software and carbon budgets for teams |
| Guides | 5 | How a capability token limits an agent, how a G-counter merges replicas, how a WebAssembly module is loaded, how hybrid key exchange combines two secrets and how a token bucket limits tool calls |
| Examples | 6 | A post-quantum inventory of settings, an agent loop with a step limit, an exhaustive check of a sort, a spec table turned into tests, a typed check for a tool call and a hash chain for an audit log |
| Projects | 4 | A to-do list that syncs two replicas, a carbon-aware job scheduler, a permission checker for agents and an SBOM writer for a lock file |
| Flex | 3 | A hash-based one-time signature, reverse-mode differentiation in 30 lines and a Hilbert curve index |
| Cross-code | 2 | Python and JavaScript merge the same counters, and C and JavaScript compute the same FNV-1a hashes |

The catalogue of database 10 (1940 to 1950) has the same ten kinds:

| Kind | Records | What it is |
|---|---:|---|
| Languages | 6 | Plankalkül, Short Code, flow diagrams for planning a program, programming ENIAC with cables and switches, the EDSAC order code and the EDVAC instruction code |
| Systems | 5 | The Automatic Computing Engine design, the Model V relay computer, the ENIAC master programmer, the IBM 604 electronic calculating punch and Shannon's communication system model |
| Devices | 8 | ENIAC, the Harvard Mark I, Colossus, the Zuse Z3 and Z4, the Manchester Baby, the IBM SSEC and the Williams–Kilburn tube |
| Tools | 6 | Paper tape on the Harvard Mark I, the IBM 602 and 603, the Friden Flexowriter, the Creed teleprinter on EDSAC and the Curta pocket calculator |
| Practices | 5 | Program and data in one store, the Moore School Lectures, the first logged bug, the imitation game as a test of machines and the Annals of the Computation Laboratory |
| Guides | 5 | How the middle-square method makes random digits, how a Hamming code corrects one bit, how a checked loop catches an off-by-one, how an ENIAC decade ring counts and how a McCulloch–Pitts neuron computes XOR |
| Examples | 6 | The Manchester Baby's first program simulated, the entropy of a short text, merge sort, matrix inversion by elimination, aliasing and a saddle point of a game |
| Projects | 4 | Colossus-style counting of agreements, the simplex method for a small linear program, a Hex winner check and Nim with the nim-sum |
| Flex | 3 | Wald's sequential test, Booth's signed multiplication and Kraft's inequality for prefix codes |
| Cross-code | 2 | C and Python compute Kaprekar's routine; C and JavaScript add odd numbers to get squares |
